# Proposed OEIS comments: A256764 to A263999

One section per OEIS entry: the date the result was obtained, the paper that proves it, what the entry still records as unsettled, and the text proposed for posting on that entry.

**Nothing here has been posted.** See [../SUBMITTING.md](../SUBMITTING.md).

---

## A256764

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A256764>  
Result obtained: **3 September 2026**  
Paper: [6945](../papers/6501-7000/6945-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 8*a(n-1) -144*a(n-3) +272*a(n-4) +704*a(n-5) -2304*a(n-6) +5120*a(n-8) -4096*a(n-9)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 73 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 6. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

> Conjecture: n*(n-3)*a(n) +2*(-4*n^2+13*n-6)*a(n-1) +(15*n^2-53*n+48)*a(n-2) +2*(2*n-3)*(n-2)*a(n-3)=0. - _R. J. Mathar_, Dec 10 2015

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
Not only empirical: the generating function given above is algebraic, and a recurrence with polynomial coefficients is a differential operator applied to it. Reducing that operator leaves a polynomial of degree 1, so the recurrence holds for n > 1. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A259216

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A259216>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [3208](../papers/3001-3500/3208-PROOF.pdf)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 9 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A259457

Proposed OEIS comment:

```

```

## A259533

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A259533>  
Result obtained: **25 August 2026**  
Paper: [44](../papers/0001-0500/0044-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Conjecture: for fixed k = 1,2,..., the sequence obtained by reducing a(n) modulo k is eventually periodic with the exact period dividing phi(k), where phi(k) is the Euler totient function A000010. For example, modulo 24 the sequence becomes [1, 4, 18, 22, 6, 22, 6, 22, 6, ...] with an apparent period of 2 beginning at a(3). - _Peter Bala_, Jul 08 2022

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
This is true. Under t = e^x - 1 the entry's e.g.f. becomes an ordinary power series with integer coefficients; that integrality kills all but finitely many terms of the Stirling expansion of a(n) modulo k, and what is left is an integer combination of the sequences n -> j^n, each eventually periodic mod k with period dividing phi(k). - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

---

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 256 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 10. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A261681

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A261681>  
Result obtained: **25 August 2026**  
Paper: [9033](../papers/9001-9500/9033-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Conjecture: -(n+1)*(n-4)*a(n) +(3*n^2-9*n-8)*a(n-1) +2*(n^2-9*n+16)*a(n-2) +4*(-3*n^2+18*n-25)*a(n-3) +8*(n-3)^2*a(n-4)=0. - _R. J. Mathar_, Jan 04 2017

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
Not only empirical: the generating function given above is algebraic, and a recurrence with polynomial coefficients is a differential operator applied to it. Reducing that operator leaves a polynomial of degree 2, so the recurrence holds for n > 2. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A262768

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A262768>  
Result obtained: **25 August 2026**  
Paper: [9141](../papers/9001-9500/9141-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Conjecture: -2*(n+2)*(49*n-95)*a(n) + 325*(n-1)*(n+1)*a(n-1) + 2*(67*n-38)*(2*n-5)*a(n-2) = 0. - _R. J. Mathar_, Jun 07 2016

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
Not only empirical: the generating function given above is algebraic, and a recurrence with polynomial coefficients is a differential operator applied to it. Reducing that operator leaves a polynomial of degree 2, so the recurrence holds for n > 2. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A263053

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A263053>  
Result obtained: **6 September 2026**  
Paper: [3222](../papers/3001-3500/3222-PROOF.pdf)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The conjectured generating function is correct. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix on 6 states, and the generating function of a walk count is 1/det(I - xM) times a polynomial, so it is a ratio of two polynomials whose degrees are at most the number of states. The conjectured expression is another such ratio, of known degree, and two of them are equal as soon as enough coefficients agree: cross-multiplying leaves a polynomial whose degree is bounded, so it vanishes once that many coefficients match. Enough coefficients were compared in exact arithmetic. It follows that a(n) satisfies the linear recurrence whose coefficients are read off the denominator, which this entry does not currently record. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 9 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 7. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A263814

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A263814>  
Result obtained: **2 September 2026**  
Paper: [176](../papers/0001-0500/0176-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical recurrence of order 82 (see link above)

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The recurrence in the linked file is correct, and it is the one below. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 1 024 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that, and Berlekamp-Massey on exact terms returns the minimal one. Its order is 82, the order stated here, and so is the order of every tail. Any recurrence of that order the sequence satisfies therefore has the same characteristic polynomial, so it is this one. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical for column k:
> Empirical for row n:

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence for column k = 1 is true. Each column counts arrays of a fixed width, and counting those a row at a time gives a transfer matrix, so every column satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most the number of states; checking the ones above is then a finite exact computation, and they hold. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```

## A263999

OEIS entry: <https://oeis.org/A263999>  
Result obtained: **1 September 2026**  
Paper: [847](../papers/0501-1000/0847-PROOF.pdf)

What the entry records as unsettled:

> Empirical: a(n) = 5*a(n-1) +37*a(n-2) -44*a(n-3) -256*a(n-4) +64*a(n-5) +256*a(n-6).
> Empirical g.f.: x*(3 + 4*x)*(6 - 5*x - 72*x^2 + 32*x^3 + 64*x^4) / (1 - 5*x - 37*x^2 + 44*x^3 + 256*x^4 - 64*x^5 - 256*x^6). - _Colin Barker_, Jan 03 2019

Proposed OEIS comment:

```
The empirical recurrence is true. Counting the arrays a row at a time gives a transfer matrix with 64 states, so a(n) satisfies a constant-coefficient linear recurrence of order at most that; checking the one above is then a finite exact computation, and it holds for n > 4. - _Adrian Perez Fontelles_, Sep 06 2026
```
